## Stadiums
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Manor_Ground,_Woolwich_Arsenal_vs._Everton.jpg)[Manor Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manor_Ground_\(Plumstead\) "Manor Ground \(Plumstead\)"), Woolwich Arsenal vs. [Everton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everton_F.C. "Everton F.C.")
Before joining the Football League, Arsenal played briefly on [Plumstead Common](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plumstead_Common "Plumstead Common"), then at the [Manor Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manor_Ground,_Plumstead "Manor Ground, Plumstead") in [Plumstead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plumstead "Plumstead"), then spent three years between 1890 and 1893 at the nearby [Invicta Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invicta_Ground "Invicta Ground"). Upon joining the Football League in 1893, the club returned to the Manor Ground and installed stands and [terracing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrace_\(stadium\) "Terrace \(stadium\)"), upgrading it from just a field. Arsenal continued to play their home games there for the next twenty years (with two exceptions in the 1894–95 season), until the move to north London in 1913.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-143)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-144)
Widely referred to as Highbury, [Arsenal Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_Stadium "Arsenal Stadium") was the club's home from September 1913 until May 2006. The original stadium was designed by the renowned football architect [Archibald Leitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archibald_Leitch "Archibald Leitch"), and had a design common to many football grounds in the UK at the time, with a single covered stand and three open-air banks of terracing.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-conservation_plan-30) The entire stadium was given a massive overhaul in the 1930s: new [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco "Art Deco") West and East stands were constructed, opening in 1932 and 1936 respectively, and a roof was added to the North Bank terrace, which was bombed during the Second World War and not restored until 1954.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-conservation_plan-30)
Highbury could hold more than 60,000 spectators at its peak, and had a capacity of 57,000 until the early 1990s. The [Taylor Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Report "Taylor Report") and Premier League regulations obliged Arsenal to convert Highbury to an all-seater stadium in time for the 1993–94 season, thus reducing the capacity to 38,419 seated spectators.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-145) This capacity had to be reduced further during [Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Champions_League "UEFA Champions League") matches to accommodate additional advertising boards, so much so that for two seasons, from 1998 to 2000, Arsenal played Champions League home matches at [Wembley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wembley_Stadium_\(1923\) "Wembley Stadium \(1923\)"), which could house more than 70,000 spectators.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-146)
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arsenal_Stadium_interior_North_Bank.jpg)The North Bank Stand, [Arsenal Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_Stadium "Arsenal Stadium"), [Highbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highbury "Highbury")
Expansion of Highbury was restricted because the East Stand had been designated as a [Grade II listed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_II_listed "Grade II listed") building and the other three stands were close to residential properties.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-conservation_plan-30) These limitations prevented the club from maximising matchday revenue during the 1990s and first decade of the 21st century, putting them in danger of being left behind in the football boom of that time.[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-147) After considering various options, in 2000 Arsenal proposed building a new 60,361-capacity stadium at Ashburton Grove, since named the [Emirates Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_Stadium "Emirates Stadium"), about 500metres south-west of Highbury.[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-148) The project was initially delayed by red tape and rising costs,[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-149) and construction was completed in July 2006, in time for the start of the 2006–07 season.[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-150) The stadium was named after its sponsors, the airline company [Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_\(airline\) "Emirates \(airline\)"), with whom the club signed the largest sponsorship deal in [English football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_England "Football in England") history, worth around £100 million.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-emiratesdeal-151) Some fans referred to the ground as Ashburton Grove, or the Grove, as they did not agree with corporate sponsorship of stadium names.[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-152) The stadium will be officially known as Emirates Stadium until at least 2028, and the airline will be the club's shirt sponsor until at least 2024.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-153)[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-154) From the start of the 2010–11 season on, the stands of the stadium have been officially known as North Bank, East Stand, West Stand and Clock end.[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-155) The capacity of the Emirates now stands at 60,704.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-cap2021-156)
Arsenal's players train at the [Shenley Training Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_Training_Centre "Arsenal Training Centre") in Hertfordshire, a purpose-built facility which opened in 1999.[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-157) Before that, the club used facilities on a nearby site owned by the [University College of London Students' Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_College_London_Union "University College London Union"). Until 1961 they had trained at Highbury.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-158) Arsenal's [Academy under-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C._Academy "Arsenal F.C. Academy") teams play their home matches at Shenley, while the [reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C._Reserves "Arsenal F.C. Reserves") play their games at [Meadow Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadow_Park_\(Borehamwood\) "Meadow Park \(Borehamwood\)"),[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-159) which is also the home of [Boreham Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borehamwood_F.C. "Borehamwood F.C."). Both the academy under-18 team and the reserves occasionally play their big games at the Emirates in front of a crowd reduced to only the lower west stand.[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-160)[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-161)
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Emirates_Stadium_-_East_stand_Club_Level.jpg "A panorama of the Emirates Stadium before a match")
[ ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Emirates_Stadium_-_East_stand_Club_Level.jpg "File:Emirates Stadium - East stand Club Level.jpg")
A [panorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panorama "Panorama") of the [Emirates Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_Stadium "Emirates Stadium") before a match
## Supporters and rivalries
Further information: [Arsenal F.C. supporters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C._supporters "Arsenal F.C. supporters")
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arsenal_Flag_\(7100433493\).jpg)Arsenal supporters
Arsenal's fanbase are referred to as "Gooners" – the name derived from the club's nickname "The Gunners". Virtually all home matches sell out; in 2007–08 Arsenal had the second-highest average League attendance for an English club (60,070, which was 99.5% of available capacity),[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-162) and, as of 2015, the third-highest all-time average attendance.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-All_Time_League_Attendance-163) In 2025 Arsenal had the fifth-highest average attendance of Premier League clubs.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-164) The club's location, adjoining wealthy areas such as [Canonbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonbury "Canonbury") and [Barnsbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barnsbury "Barnsbury"), mixed areas such as [Islington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islington "Islington"), [Holloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holloway,_London "Holloway, London"), [Highbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highbury "Highbury"), and the adjacent [London Borough of Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Borough_of_Camden "London Borough of Camden"), and largely working-class areas such as [Finsbury Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finsbury_Park_\(district\) "Finsbury Park \(district\)") and [Stoke Newington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoke_Newington "Stoke Newington"), has meant that Arsenal's supporters have come from a variety of social classes. Much of the Afro-Caribbean support comes from the neighbouring [London Borough of Hackney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Borough_of_Hackney "London Borough of Hackney") and a large portion of the South Asian Arsenal supporters commute to the stadium from [Wembley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wembley_Park "Wembley Park"), North West of the capital. There is also traditionally a large Irish community that followed Arsenal, with the surrounding Islington and particularly the nearby [Archway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archway,_London "Archway, London") area having a large community of residents with Irish heritage.
Like all major [English football clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Football_Clubs "English Football Clubs"), Arsenal have a number of domestic supporters' clubs, including the Arsenal Football Supporters' Club, which works closely with the club, and the Arsenal Independent Supporters' Association, which maintains a more independent line. The [Arsenal Supporters' Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_Supporters'_Trust "Arsenal Supporters' Trust") promotes greater participation in ownership of the club by fans. The club's supporters also publish [fanzines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanzine "Fanzine") such as _The Gooner_ , _Gunflash_ and the satirical _Up The Arse!_
There have always been Arsenal supporters outside London, and since the advent of satellite television, a supporter's attachment to a football club has become less dependent on geography. Consequently, Arsenal have a significant number of fans from beyond London and all over the world; in 2007, 24 UK, 37 Irish and 49 other overseas supporters' clubs were affiliated with the club.[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-165) A 2011 report by SPORT+MARKT estimated Arsenal's global fanbase at 113million.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-SPORT+MARKT_analysis-166) The club is especially popular in Africa, owing to many of its players, past and present, such as those from the Wenger era, coming from Africa or being descendants from west Africa and the Caribbean.[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-167) As a homage to this support, the 2024–25 alternative away kit used black, red and green to refer to the [pan-African flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-African_flag "Pan-African flag").[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-168) In 2025 the club's social media activity was the fifth-highest among English football clubs.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-169)
### Chants
The team's anthem is "[The Angel (North London Forever)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Angel_\(North_London_Forever\) "The Angel \(North London Forever\)")" by [Louis Dunford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Dunford "Louis Dunford").[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-170)[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-171)[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-172) The song is typically played at Arsenal home games before a match.
In addition to the usual English [football chants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_chant "Football chant"), Arsenal's supporters sing "One-Nil to the Arsenal" (to the tune of "[Go West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go_West_\(song\) "Go West \(song\)")") and also regularly sing "Who's that team they call the Arsenal", "[Good Old Arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Old_Arsenal "Good Old Arsenal")" (to the tune of "[Rule, Britannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule,_Britannia "Rule, Britannia")!") and "We're the North Bank/Clock End Highbury". The fans also chant "Boring, Boring Arsenal" in self-deprecating reference to Arsenal's reputation during the 1970s and 1980s as an overly defensive, cautious team.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:6-173)
### Rivalries
Main articles: [North London derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_London_derby "North London derby"), [Arsenal F.C.–Manchester United F.C. rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.%E2%80%93Manchester_United_F.C._rivalry "Arsenal F.C.–Manchester United F.C. rivalry"), [Arsenal F.C.–Chelsea F.C. rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.%E2%80%93Chelsea_F.C._rivalry "Arsenal F.C.–Chelsea F.C. rivalry"), and [Arsenal F.C.–Manchester City F.C. rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.%E2%80%93Manchester_City_F.C._rivalry "Arsenal F.C.–Manchester City F.C. rivalry")
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Laurent_Koscielny_clashes_with_Heurelho_Gomes.jpg)Arsenal playing against rivals Tottenham, in a game known as the [North London derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_London_derby "North London derby"), in November 2010
Arsenal's longest-running and deepest rivalry is with their nearest major neighbour, [Tottenham Hotspur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tottenham_Hotspur_F.C. "Tottenham Hotspur F.C."); matches between the two are referred to as the [North London derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_London_derby "North London derby").[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-174) There also exists a rivalry between Arsenal and [Chelsea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_F.C. "Chelsea F.C."). In addition, Arsenal and Manchester United developed a strong on-pitch rivalry in the late 1980s, which intensified in the early 2000s when both clubs were competing for the Premier League title.[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-175)[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-176)[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-177)
## Mascot
The club mascot is Gunnersaurus Rex, a smiling, seven-foot-tall green dinosaur, who first appeared at a home match against Manchester City in August 1994 (or 1993). He is based on a drawing by then-11-year-old Peter Lovell, whose design and another similar idea won a Junior Gunners contest; his official backstory is that he hatched from an egg found during renovations at Highbury.[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-178)[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-179)[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-180)
The same performer, Jerry Quy, has been inside the suit from the start; in early October 2020, as part of cost-cutting brought about by the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_England "COVID-19 pandemic in England"), the club [made him redundant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Layoff "Layoff") from that and his other part-time job in supporter liaison, together with 55 full-time employees, although they later said Gunnersaurus could return after spectators were allowed back in stadiums.[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Ind-181)[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-182)[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-continue-183) An online fundraiser was begun for Quy,[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-continue-183) and [Mesut Özil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesut_%C3%96zil "Mesut Özil") offered to pay his salary himself as long as he remains with Arsenal.[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-NYT-184)[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-185) In November 2020, in advance of COVID-19 regulations being relaxed to allow supporters to attend home games from 3 December, Arsenal announced that Gunnersaurus would return, to be played by a roster of people that could include Quy if he wished.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-186)[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-187)
## Ownership and finances
The largest shareholder on the Arsenal board is American sports tycoon [Stan Kroenke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Kroenke "Stan Kroenke").[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Arsenal_Board-188) Kroenke first launched a bid for the club in April 2007,[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-kroenke-189) and faced competition for shares from Red and White Securities, which acquired its first shares from [David Dein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Dein "David Dein") in August 2007.[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-usmanov-190) Red & White Securities was co-owned by Russian billionaire [Alisher Usmanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alisher_Usmanov "Alisher Usmanov") and London-based Iranian financier [Farhad Moshiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farhad_Moshiri_\(businessman\) "Farhad Moshiri \(businessman\)"), though Usmanov bought Moshiri's stake in 2016.[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Usmanov_buys_Moshiri-191) Kroenke came close to the 30% takeover threshold in November 2009, when he increased his holding to 18,594 shares (29.9%).[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-192)[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Kroenke_at_threshold-193) In April 2011, Kroenke achieved a full takeover by purchasing the shareholdings of [Nina Bracewell-Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nina_Bracewell-Smith "Nina Bracewell-Smith") and [Danny Fiszman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danny_Fiszman "Danny Fiszman"), taking his shareholding to 62.89%.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Kroenke_buys_Arsenal,_BBC-194)[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Kroenke_buys_Arsenal,_Guardian-195) In May 2017, Kroenke owned 41,721 shares (67.05%) and Red & White Securities owned 18,695 shares (30.04%).[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Arsenal_Board-188) In January 2018, Kroenke expanded his ownership by buying twenty-two more shares, taking his total ownership to 67.09%.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-196) In August 2018, Kroenke bought out Usmanov for £550m. Now owning more than 90% of the shares, he had the required stake to complete the buyout of the remaining shares and become the sole owner.[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-197) There has been criticism of Arsenal's poor performance since Kroenke took over, which has been attributed to his ownership.[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-198) [Ivan Gazidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Gazidis "Ivan Gazidis") was the club's Chief executive from 2009 to 2018.[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Arsenal_Board-188)[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-199)
Arsenal's parent company, Arsenal Holdings plc, operates as an unlisted [public limited company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_limited_company "Public limited company"), whose ownership is considerably different from that of other football clubs. Only 62,219 shares in Arsenal have been issued,[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Arsenal_Board-188) and they are not traded on a public exchange such as the [FTSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FTSE_Group "FTSE Group") or [AIM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_Investment_Market "Alternative Investment Market"); instead, they are traded relatively infrequently on the [ICAP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICAP_\(company\) "ICAP \(company\)") Securities and Derivatives Exchange, a specialist market. On 29 May 2017, a single share in Arsenal had a [mid price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid_price "Mid price") of £18,000, which sets the club's [market capitalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalisation "Market capitalisation") value at approximately £1,119.9m.[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Arsenal,_NEX_Exchange-200) Most football clubs are not listed on an exchange, which makes direct comparisons of their values difficult. Consultants Brand Finance valued the club's brand and intangible assets at $703m in 2015, and consider Arsenal an AAA global brand.[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-World_Football_Brands-201) Business magazine _[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes "Forbes")_ [valued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes'_list_of_the_most_valuable_football_clubs "Forbes' list of the most valuable football clubs") Arsenal as a whole at $2.238 billion (£1.69 billion) in 2018, ranked third in English football.[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Forbes2018-202) Research by the [Henley Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henley_Business_School "Henley Business School") ranked Arsenal second in English football, modelling the club's value at £1.118billion in 2015.[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Markham_2015-203)[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-MMM-204)
Arsenal's financial results for the [2019–20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Premier_League "2019–20 Premier League") season showed an after tax loss of £47.8m, due in part to the impact of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic").[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-205) The [Deloitte Football Money League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deloitte_Football_Money_League "Deloitte Football Money League") is a publication that homogenises and compares clubs' annual revenue. Deloitte put Arsenal's footballing revenue in 2019 at £392.7m (€445.6m),[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-cityam1-206) ranking Arsenal eleventh among world football clubs.[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Deloitte_Football_Money_League-207) Arsenal and [Deloitte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deloitte "Deloitte") both listed the match day revenue generated in 2019 by the Emirates Stadium as €109.2m (£96.2m).[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-cityam1-206)
## In popular culture
Partly due to their proximity to the [Alexandra Palace transmitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandra_Palace_television_station "Alexandra Palace television station"), Arsenal have appeared in a number of media "firsts". On 22 January 1927, their match at Highbury against [Sheffield United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_United_F.C. "Sheffield United F.C.") was the first English League match to be broadcast live on radio.[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstsLasts-208)[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-209) A decade later, on 16 September 1937, an exhibition match between Arsenal's first team and the reserves was the first football match in the world to be televised live.[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstsLasts-208)[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-210) Arsenal also featured in the first edition of the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC")'s _[Match of the Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Match_of_the_Day "Match of the Day")_ , which screened highlights of their match against Liverpool at [Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield "Anfield") on 22 August 1964.[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstsLasts-208)[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-bbchist-211) [Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_UK "Sky UK")'s coverage of Arsenal's January 2010 match against [Manchester United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_United_F.C. "Manchester United F.C.") was the first live public broadcast of a sports event on [3D television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D_television "3D television").[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstsLasts-208)[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-212)
As one of the most successful teams in the country, Arsenal have often featured when football is depicted in the arts in Britain. They formed the backdrop to one of the earliest football-related novels, _[The Arsenal Stadium Mystery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Arsenal_Stadium_Mystery_\(novel\) "The Arsenal Stadium Mystery \(novel\)")_ (1939), which was [made into a film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Arsenal_Stadium_Mystery "The Arsenal Stadium Mystery") in the same year.[[208]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-213) The story centres on a [friendly match](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friendly_match "Friendly match") between Arsenal and an amateur side, one of whose players is poisoned while playing. Many Arsenal players appeared as themselves in the film and manager [George Allison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Allison "George Allison") was given a speaking part.[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-atthemovies-214) The book _[Fever Pitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fever_Pitch "Fever Pitch")_ by [Nick Hornby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Hornby "Nick Hornby") was an autobiographical account of Hornby's life and relationship with football, and with Arsenal in particular.[[210]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-215) Published in 1992, it formed part of the revival and rehabilitation of football in British society during the 1990s.[[211]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-216) The book was twice adapted for the cinema–the [1997 British film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fever_Pitch_\(1997_film\) "Fever Pitch \(1997 film\)") focuses on Arsenal's 1988–89 title win, and a [2005 American version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fever_Pitch_\(2005_film\) "Fever Pitch \(2005 film\)") features a fan of baseball's [Boston Red Sox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Red_Sox "Boston Red Sox").[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-217)
Arsenal have often been stereotyped as a [defensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defender_\(association_football\) "Defender \(association football\)") and "boring" side, especially during the 1970s and 1980s.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:6-173)[[213]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-218) In the 1997 film _[The Full Monty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Full_Monty "The Full Monty")_ the principal characters move forward in a line and raise their hands, deliberately mimicking the Arsenal defence's [offside trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offside_trap "Offside trap"), in an attempt to co-ordinate their [striptease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striptease "Striptease") routine.[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-atthemovies-214) Fifteen years later, an almost identical scene was included in the 2012 Disney science-fiction film _[John Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Carter_\(film\) "John Carter \(film\)")_ (director and co-writer [Andrew Stanton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Stanton "Andrew Stanton"), a notable overseas supporter of the club), along with other visual cues and oblique dialogue hints and references to the club throughout the film.[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-219) Another film reference to the club's defence comes in the film _[Plunkett& Macleane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plunkett_&_Macleane "Plunkett & Macleane")_, in which two characters are named Dixon and Winterburn after Arsenal's long-serving full backs – the right-sided [Lee Dixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Dixon "Lee Dixon") and the left-sided [Nigel Winterburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Winterburn "Nigel Winterburn").[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-atthemovies-214)
In August 2022, [Amazon Prime Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_Prime_Video "Amazon Prime Video") released an eight-episode [docuseries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Documentary_film "Documentary film") called _[All or Nothing: Arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_or_Nothing:_Arsenal "All or Nothing: Arsenal")_.[[215]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-220)[[216]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-221) It documented the club by spending time with the coaching staff and players behind the scenes both on and off the field throughout their [2021–22 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%9322_Arsenal_F.C._season "2021–22 Arsenal F.C. season"), in which they were the youngest team in the Premier League with an average starting age of 24 years and 308 days – more than a whole year younger than the next team.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:4-107)[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:5-108)
## In the community
In 1985, Arsenal founded a [community scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_social_responsibility "Corporate social responsibility"), "Arsenal in the Community", which offered sporting, [social inclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_inclusion "Social inclusion"), educational and charitable projects. The club support a number of charitable causes directly and in 1992 established The Arsenal Charitable Trust, which by 2006 had raised more than £2 million for local causes.[[217]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-222) An ex-professional and celebrity football team associated with the club also raised money by playing charity matches.[[218]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-community-223) The club launched the Arsenal for Everyone initiative in 2008 as an annual celebration of the diversity of the Arsenal family.[[219]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-224) In the 2009–10 season Arsenal announced that they had raised a record breaking £818,897 for the [Great Ormond Street Hospital Children's Charity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Ormond_Street_Hospital_Children's_Charity "Great Ormond Street Hospital Children's Charity"). The original target was £500,000.[[220]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-225) In 2022, Arsenal and Adidas partnered up to launch the "No More Red" campaign to support the long-standing work being done by Arsenal in the Community to help keep young people safe from knife crime and youth violence. To promote the event, the club launched an exclusive all white kit that was not commercially available and only awarded to individuals who are making a positive difference in the community.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-226)
[Save the Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_the_Children "Save the Children") has been Arsenal global charity partner since 2011 and have worked together in numerous projects to improve safety and well-being for vulnerable children in London and abroad. On 3 September 2016 The Arsenal Foundation has donated £1m to build football pitches for children in London, Indonesia, Iraq, Jordan and Somalia thanks to The Arsenal Foundation Legends Match against Milan Glorie at the Emirates Stadium.[[222]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-227) On 3 June 2018, Arsenal played Real Madrid in the Corazon Classic Match 2018 at the Bernabeu, where the proceeds went to Realtoo Real Madrid Foundation projects that are aimed at the most vulnerable children. In addition there will be a return meeting on 8 September 2018 at the Emirates stadium where proceeds will go towards the Arsenal foundation.[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-228)
During 2007 in [Pleiku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleiku "Pleiku"), Vietnam, Arsenal partnered with the [JMG Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JMG_Academy "JMG Academy") and the [Hoang Anh Gia Lai Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoang_Anh_Gia_Lai_Group "Hoang Anh Gia Lai Group") to found a [youth academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoang_Anh_Gia_Lai_Academy "Hoang Anh Gia Lai Academy") for the [V.League 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V.League_1 "V.League 1") side [Hoàng Anh Gia Lai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoang_Anh_Gia_Lai_FC "Hoang Anh Gia Lai FC"),[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-229) which saw a selection of Vietnam-based players train with Arsenal;[[225]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-230) the club ended their partnership with the club in 2017.[[226]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-231) Additionally, the club formally partnered with a variety of clubs overseas including Virginia based [Richmond Strikers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond_Strikers "Richmond Strikers") and Cairo based [Wadi Degla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Degla_SC "Wadi Degla SC").[[227]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:0-232)[[228]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-233)

## Management and staff
See also: [List of Arsenal F.C. managers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arsenal_F.C._managers "List of Arsenal F.C. managers")
### Current staff
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mikel_Arteta_2021_\(cropped\).png)Having served as Arsenal's club captain during his playing career, Arteta was appointed as manager in December 2019.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:3-101) [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arsene-Wenger.jpg)[Arsène Wenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ars%C3%A8ne_Wenger "Arsène Wenger") managed Arsenal from 1996 to 2018; he is the club's longest serving manager.[[233]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-238)

## Statistics and records
Further information: [List of Arsenal F.C. records and statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arsenal_F.C._records_and_statistics "List of Arsenal F.C. records and statistics")
[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Thierry_Henry_applauding_2012.jpg)[Thierry Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thierry_Henry "Thierry Henry") is Arsenal's record goalscorer, with 228 goals in all competitions.[[241]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-henry-246)
Arsenal's tally of 14 League Championships is the [third highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_English_football_champions#Total_titles_won "List of English football champions") in English football, after Liverpool (20) and Manchester United (20),[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:8-247) and they were the first club [to reach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_English_football_champions#Football_League_First_Division_\(1892%E2%80%931992\) "List of English football champions") a seventh and an eighth League Championship. As of June 2020, they are one of seven teams, the others being Manchester United, [Blackburn Rovers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackburn_Rovers_F.C. "Blackburn Rovers F.C."), [Chelsea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_F.C. "Chelsea F.C."), [Manchester City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_City_F.C. "Manchester City F.C."), [Leicester City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leicester_City_F.C. "Leicester City F.C.") and Liverpool, to have won the Premier League since its formation in 1992.[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:8-247)
They hold the highest number of FA Cup trophies, with 14.[[243]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:7-248) The club is one of only six clubs to have won the FA Cup twice in succession, in 2002 and 2003, and 2014 and 2015.[[243]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:7-248) Arsenal have achieved three League and FA Cup "[Doubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_\(association_football\)#England "Double \(association football\)")" (in 1971, 1998 and 2002), a feat only previously achieved by Manchester United (in 1994, 1996 and 1999).[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FCHD-84)[[244]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-249) They were the first side in English football to complete the FA Cup and League Cup double, in 1993.[[245]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-250) Arsenal were also the first London club to reach the final of the UEFA Champions League, in 2006, losing the final 2–1 to [Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FC_Barcelona "FC Barcelona")[[246]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-251) and they lost the Champions League final again, in 2026, 3-4 on penalties against [Paris Saint-Germain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Saint-Germain "Paris Saint-Germain"), after a 1-1 draw.[[247]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Reuters2026Final-252)
Arsenal have one of the best top-flight records in history, having finished below fourteenth only seven times. They have won the second most top flight league matches in English football, and have also accumulated the second most points,[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-RSSSF1stLevelAllTime-5) whether calculated by two points per win[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-RSSSF1stLevelAllTime-5) or by the contemporary points value.[[248]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-All_Time_Table-253) They have been in the top flight for the most consecutive seasons (98 as of 2023–24).[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-RSSSF_Div_Movements-4)[[249]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-All-time_Arsenal-254)[[250]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-255) Arsenal also have the highest average league finishing position for the 20th century, with an average league placement of 8.5.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Independent:_Hodgson-3)
Arsenal hold the record for the longest run of unbeaten League matches (49 between May 2003 and October 2004).[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-49unbeaten-83) This included all 38 matches of their title-winning [2003–04](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003%E2%80%9304_in_English_football "2003–04 in English football") season, when Arsenal became only the second club to finish a top-flight campaign unbeaten, after [Preston North End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston_North_End_F.C. "Preston North End F.C.") (who played only 22 matches) in [1888–89](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1888%E2%80%9389_in_English_football "1888–89 in English football").[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-invincibles-82)[[251]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-49_Unbeaten-256) They also hold the record for the longest top flight win streak.[[252]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-EPL_Records-257) Arsenal set a Champions League record during the 2005–06 season by going ten matches without conceding a goal, beating the previous best of seven set by [AC Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AC_Milan "AC Milan"). They went a record total stretch of 995minutes without letting an opponent score; the streak ended in [the final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_UEFA_Champions_League_Final "2006 UEFA Champions League Final"), when [Samuel Eto'o](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Eto'o "Samuel Eto'o") scored a 76th-minute equaliser for Barcelona.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-2006ucl-85)
[David O'Leary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_O'Leary "David O'Leary") holds the record for Arsenal appearances, having played 722 first-team matches between 1975 and 1993. Fellow [centre half](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_half "Centre half") and former captain [Tony Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Adams_\(footballer\) "Tony Adams \(footballer\)") comes second, having played 669 times. The record for a [goalkeeper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goalkeeper_\(association_football\) "Goalkeeper \(association football\)") is held by [David Seaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Seaman "David Seaman"), with 564 appearances.[[253]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-clubrecords-258) [Thierry Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thierry_Henry "Thierry Henry") is the club's top goalscorer with 228 goals in all competitions between 1999 and 2012;[[241]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-henry-246) he surpassed [Ian Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Wright "Ian Wright")'s total of 185 in October 2005.[[254]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-259) Wright's record had stood since September 1997, when he overtook the longstanding total of 178 goals set by winger [Cliff Bastin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cliff_Bastin "Cliff Bastin") in 1939.[[255]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-260) Henry also holds the club record for goals scored in the League, with 175,[[241]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-henry-246) a record that had been held by Bastin until February 2006.[[256]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-261) [Declan Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declan_Rice "Declan Rice") holds the Arsenal record signing price after a deal with [West Ham United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Ham_United "West Ham United") was completed in July 2023, for an initial £100 million. This easily surpassed the former record of £72 million for [Nicolas Pepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Pepe "Nicolas Pepe").[[257]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-262)
Arsenal's record home attendance is 73,707, for a [UEFA Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Champions_League "UEFA Champions League") match against [Lens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RC_Lens "RC Lens") on 25 November 1998 at [Wembley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wembley_Stadium_\(1923\) "Wembley Stadium \(1923\)"), where the club formerly played home European matches because of the limits on Highbury's capacity. The record attendance for an Arsenal match at Highbury is 73,295, for a 0–0 draw against [Sunderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunderland_A.F.C. "Sunderland A.F.C.") on 9 March 1935,[[253]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-clubrecords-258) while that at Emirates Stadium is 60,161, for a 2–2 draw with Manchester United on 3 November 2007.[[258]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-263)

## Honours
Further information: [List of Arsenal F.C. seasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arsenal_F.C._seasons "List of Arsenal F.C. seasons")
For honours won by Academy teams, see [Arsenal F.C. Under-23s and Academy §Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C._Under-23s_and_Academy#Honours "Arsenal F.C. Under-23s and Academy").
Arsenal's first ever [silverware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silverware_\(Sport\) "Silverware \(Sport\)") was won as the Royal Arsenal in 1890. The [Kent Junior Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Football_Association#County_Cup_Competitions "Kent County Football Association"), won by Royal Arsenal's reserves, was the club's first trophy, while the first team's first trophy came three weeks later when they won the [Kent Senior Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_Senior_Cup "Kent Senior Cup").[[259]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-264)[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-aftlu-265) Their first national senior honour came in 1930, when they won the [FA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FA_Cup "FA Cup").[[261]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-266) The club enjoyed further success in the 1930s, winning another FA Cup and five [Football League First Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_League_First_Division "Football League First Division") titles.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-:1-52)[[262]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-267) Arsenal won their first league and cup [double](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_\(association_football\)#England "Double \(association football\)") in the [1970–71 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970%E2%80%9371_in_English_football "1970–71 in English football") and twice repeated the feat, in [1997–98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997%E2%80%9398_in_English_football "1997–98 in English football") and [2001–02](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001%E2%80%9302_in_English_football "2001–02 in English football"), as well as winning a cup double of the FA Cup and [League Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_League_Cup "Football League Cup") in [1992–93](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992%E2%80%9393_in_English_football "1992–93 in English football").[[263]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-268) The [2003–04](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003%E2%80%9304_Arsenal_F.C._season "2003–04 Arsenal F.C. season") season was the only 38-match league season [unbeaten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Invincibles_\(football\) "The Invincibles \(football\)") in English football history. A special gold version of the Premier League trophy was commissioned and presented to the club the following season.[[264]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-269)

### Other
When the FA Cup was the only national [football association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_association "Football association") competition available to Arsenal, the other football association competitions were [County Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_football_association "County football association"), and they made up many of the matches the club played during a season.[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-aftlu-265) Arsenal's first first-team trophy was a County Cup, the inaugural [Kent Senior Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_Senior_Cup "Kent Senior Cup").[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstCountyCup-18) Arsenal became ineligible for the [London Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Football_Association "London Football Association") when the club turned professional in 1891, and rarely participated in County Cups after this.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-TurnProfessional-20)[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstSuperstar-274) Due to the club's original location within the borders of both the London and [Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_County_Football_Association "Kent County Football Association") Football Associations,[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-275) Arsenal competed in and won trophies organised by each.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstCountyCup-18)[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FirstSuperstar-274)
During Arsenal's history, the club has participated in and won a variety of pre-season and friendly honours. These include Arsenal's own pre-season competition the [Emirates Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_Cup "Emirates Cup"), begun in 2007.[[267]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-276) During the wars, previous competitions were widely suspended and the club had to participate in wartime competitions. [During WWII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football_during_World_War_II "Association football during World War II"), Arsenal won several of these.
## Notes
1. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-16 "Jump up") Woolwich and Plumstead were officially part of [Kent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent "Kent") until the creation of the [County of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_London "County of London") in 1889. _The Arsenal History_ provides primary sources on the name, first meeting, and first match.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-13) [Bernard Joy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Joy "Bernard Joy") says Danskin was captain at founding.[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJoy20092-14) Danskin was made official captain the next month.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-Royal_Arsenal_name-15)
2. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-42 "Jump up") The new shirts are exhibited in _The Arsenal Shirt_.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElkinShakeshaft2014-39) Newspaper accounts of the addition of white sleeves are provided by Mark Andrews.[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-40) The contemporary discussion around the first use of shirt numbers, and its initial trial by [Chelsea F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_F.C. "Chelsea F.C."), is provided by Neil Glackin.[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-41)
3. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-65 "Jump up") [Martin Keown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Keown "Martin Keown") was the 'fifth' member of the Back Four, but did not play for the club between 1986 and 1993.
4. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-76 "Jump up") These changes have received contemporary attention,[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-72) and later praise[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-FOOTNOTECross2015-73) and scepticism.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-74) For context of the broader use of science in English football, see _Soccer Science_.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-75)
5. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-81 "Jump up") Several analyses indicate strong league performance across the Wenger period, given Arsenal's footballing outlays, including a [regression analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regression_analysis "Regression analysis") on wage bills,[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-77) regression on transfer spending,[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-78) regression on both,[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-79) and a [bootstrapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bootstrapping_\(statistics\) "Bootstrapping \(statistics\)") approach for the period 2004–09.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_note-80)
6. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-270 "Jump up") Upon its formation in 1992, the [Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League "Premier League") became the top tier of [English football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_England "Football in England"); the [Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Football_League "English Football League") [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_League_First_Division "Football League First Division") and [Second Divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_League_Second_Division "Football League Second Division") then became the second and third tiers, respectively. From 2004, the First Division became the [Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EFL_Championship "EFL Championship") and the Second Division became [League One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EFL_League_One "EFL League One").
7. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-271 "Jump up") Until 2016, the unsponsored name of the EFL Cup was the Football League Cup.
8. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-272 "Jump up") Until 2002, the FA Community Shield was known as the FA Charity Shield.
9. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenal_F.C.#cite_ref-273 "Jump up") The 1991 FA Charity Shield was shared with Tottenham Hotspur.
